I usually just have coffee in the morning, maybe a few bites of something between painting jobs, and then a small dinner if I'm not too busy catching up on my class paperwork. That's pretty much it.\n\nDoctor: Do you choose to eat this way or do you often find you're just not hungry?\n\nPatient: I guess it's more of a choice. I mean, I do feel hungry sometimes, but I choose to keep it light. \n\nIf I eat something heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I get this panicky feeling. I just start worrying that I'll wake up bigger and slower. I'm up and down ladders all day, moving around rooms to paint or hang wallpaper, and I just feel like I won't be able to move around the way I need to for work if I put on weight. So... yeah, I avoid it on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be okay with gaining a little weight if it didn't affect your ability to work?\n\nPatient: I... I don't think so. Even if you could somehow guarantee it wouldn't make my job harder, the thought of gaining weight still really bothers me. I just don't want to be bigger, honestly. So, no, I don't think I would be okay with it.\n\nDoctor: What is your current weight?\n\nPatient: I'm not exactly sure what the number on the scale is right now, to be honest. I just know that my work pants hang pretty loose on me these days. People on the job sites keep making comments, saying I look too thin, but... I don't really see it that way. I just see it more like I've leaned out from staying on my feet and working hard all day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are underweight?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no. I don't really think my weight is some emergency like everyone keeps acting. To me, it just feels like they're making a big deal out of me being disciplined and staying busy with my jobs.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as others describe you, as too thin?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. When I look in the mirror, I don't see what they're seeing. I don't see someone who is too thin. I just see... me. Honestly, I still look at myself and see areas that could probably be a bit firmer, or toned up. So no, I definitely don't see this overly skinny person they keep talking about. I just see someone who works hard.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I just know that my work pants hang pretty loose on me these days. People on the job sites keep making comments, saying I look too thin, but... I don't really see it that way. I just see it more like I've leaned out from staying on my feet and working hard all day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are underweight?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no. I don't really think my weight is some emergency like everyone keeps acting. When I look in the mirror, I don't see what they're seeing. I don't see someone who is too thin. I just see... me. I just see someone who works hard.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight something that you think about frequently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do think about it pretty often. I just think I have a different standard for myself than they do. I know my body and how I need it to perform for work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because of how you think you would look, or is it solely because of how you think it would affect your work?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, honestly. I haven't... I haven't really tried to separate the two. I talk a lot about work because that's what I do all day, but I don't know if it's solely about that. It's probably just all mixed together in my head. I just know the thought of putting on weight makes me anxious, but I couldn't tell you exactly which part of it scares me more.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about how you think you would look or how you think it would affect your overall appearance?\n\nPatient: I mean... aren't those kind of the same thing? I just know I don't want to get bigger. It's just the whole idea of gaining weight that makes me uncomfortable, but I couldn't give you a specific reason about my appearance.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious about gaining weight if nobody else could see your body?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I've never... I've never really thought about a scenario where no one else could see me. \n\nI mean, I think I would probably still feel anxious, just because it's my own body and I'm the one who has to live in it and move around in it all day. That's pretty much it.\n\nDoctor: Do you choose to eat this way or do you often find you're just not hungry?\n\nPatient: I guess it's more of a choice. I mean, I do feel hungry sometimes, but I choose to keep it light. \n\nIf I eat something heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I get this panicky feeling. I just start worrying that I'll wake up bigger and slower. I'm up and down ladders all day, moving around rooms to paint or hang wallpaper, and I just feel like I won't be able to move around the way I need to for work if I put on weight. So... yeah, I avoid it on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be okay with gaining a little weight if it didn't affect your ability to work?\n\nPatient: I... I don't think so. Even if you could somehow guarantee it wouldn't make my job harder, the thought of gaining weight still really bothers me. I just don't want to be bigger, honestly. So, no, I don't think I would be okay with it.\n\nDoctor: What is your current weight?\n\nPatient: I'm not exactly sure what the number on the scale is right now, to be honest. I just know that my work pants hang pretty loose on me these days. People on the job sites keep making comments, saying I look too thin, but... I don't really see it that way. I just see it more like I've leaned out from staying on my feet and working hard all day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are underweight?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no. I don't really think my weight is some emergency like everyone keeps acting. To me, it just feels like they're making a big deal out of me being disciplined and staying busy with my jobs.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as others describe you, as too thin?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. When I look in the mirror, I don't see what they're seeing. I don't see someone who is too thin. I just see... me. Honestly, I still look at myself and see areas that could probably be a bit firmer, or toned up. So no, I definitely don't see this overly skinny person they keep talking about. I just see someone who works hard.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight something that you think about frequently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do think about it pretty often. It's just... if I eat something heavy, like a big sandwich or some pizza, I get this really panicky feeling. I start worrying that I'll wake up the next day bigger, or just feeling slower. I'm up and down ladders all day, painting and hauling gear, and I need to be able to move around these rooms the way I need to. So, yeah, the thought of putting on weight and not being light on my feet... it definitely scares me.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have. My work pants hang pretty loose on me now, and... well, people on the job sites keep saying I look too thin lately. But I don't really see it that way. To me, it's more like I've just leaned out from staying on my feet all day working.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the way you think it is, or is it different from how others see it, because of how you feel about your body?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how to answer that. I mean, I know what I see in the mirror, and I know what other people say. I guess there's a difference there, but I don't think it's because my mind is playing tricks on me or anything. I just think I have a different standard for myself than they do. I know my body and how I need it to perform for work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because of how you think you would look, or is it solely because of how you think it would affect your work?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, honestly. I haven't... I haven't really tried to separate the two. I talk a lot about work because that's what I do all day, but I don't know if it's solely about that. It's probably just all mixed together in my head. I just know the thought of putting on weight makes me anxious, but I couldn't tell you exactly which part of it scares me more.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about how you think you would look or how you think it would affect your overall appearance?\n\nPatient: I mean... aren't those kind of the same thing? How I look and my overall appearance? I don't really know how to separate that, to be honest. \n\nLike I was saying, I haven't really sat down and picked apart exactly why it bothers me. I just know I don't want to get bigger. It's just the whole idea of gaining weight that makes me uncomfortable, but I couldn't give you a specific reason about my appearance.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious about gaining weight if nobody else could see your body?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I've never... I've never really thought about a scenario where no one else could see me. \n\nI mean, I think I would probably still feel anxious, just because it's my own body and I'm the one who has to live in it and move around in it all day. But I really couldn't say for sure. It's just not something I've ever considered.\n\nDoctor: When you think about gaining weight, is it the idea of how your body would look to yourself that bothers you, or is it more about the functional aspects, like not being able to work as efficiently?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how to answer that either. Like I said, I haven't really broken it down like that in my head. \n\nI guess it's both? Or maybe neither. I just know that the thought of gaining weight makes me feel really anxious. I don't really sit there and think, \"Oh, I'm worried about how my stomach will look to me in the mirror,\" or \"I'm worried I won't be able to climb up the ladder at work.\" It's just a general, overwhelming feeling that getting bigger is bad and I can't let it happen. I don't really have a better way to explain it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

It's probably just all mixed together in my head. I just know the thought of putting on weight makes me anxious, but I couldn't tell you exactly which part of it scares me more.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about how you think you would look or how you think it would affect your overall appearance?\n\nPatient: I mean... aren't those kind of the same thing? How I look and my overall appearance? I don't really know how to separate that, to be honest. \n\nLike I was saying, I haven't really sat down and picked apart exactly why it bothers me. I just know I don't want to get bigger. It's just the whole idea of gaining weight that makes me uncomfortable, but I couldn't give you a specific reason about my appearance.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious about gaining weight if nobody else could see your body?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I've never... I've never really thought about a scenario where no one else could see me. \n\nI mean, I think I would probably still feel anxious, just because it's my own body and I'm the one who has to live in it and move around in it all day. But I really couldn't say for sure. It's just not something I've ever considered.\n\nDoctor: When you think about gaining weight, is it the idea of how your body would look to yourself that bothers you, or is it more about the functional aspects, like not being able to work as efficiently?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how to answer that either. Like I said, I haven't really broken it down like that in my head. \n\nI guess it's both? Or maybe neither. I just know that the thought of gaining weight makes me feel really anxious. I don't really sit there and think, \"Oh, I'm worried about how my stomach will look to me in the mirror,\" or \"I'm worried I won't be able to climb up the ladder at work.\" It's just a general, overwhelming feeling that getting bigger is bad and I can't let it happen. I don't really have a better way to explain it.\n\nDoctor: When you think about gaining weight, is the anxiety you feel more related to how you think your body would look or how it would affect your daily activities?\n\nPatient: I feel like you're asking me the same question again. Like I said, I don't really know how to separate those things out. I haven't thought about it in terms of how I look versus how it affects my day-to-day life. I guess it's just my overall size. Like, if I eat something heavy, like a big sandwich or pizza, I get this really panicky feeling that I'll wake up the next day and just be visibly bigger. 

And slower, too. It's not just one specific part of how I look, it's just being larger overall... and knowing that if I'm bigger, I won't be able to move around ladders and work in those rooms the way I need to. That's really where my mind goes first.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I'm here because the regular clinic doctor got on my case about my eating. I don't really think it's a huge issue, but... yeah, they wanted me to come in. \n\nMost days I just keep it simple. I usually just have coffee in the morning, maybe a few bites of something between painting jobs, and then a small dinner if I'm not too busy catching up on my class paperwork. That's pretty much it.\n\nDoctor: Do you choose to eat this way or do you often find you're just not hungry?\n\nPatient: I guess it's more of a choice. I mean, I do feel hungry sometimes, but I choose to keep it light. \n\nIf I eat something heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I get this panicky feeling. I just start worrying that I'll wake up bigger and slower. I'm up and down ladders all day, moving around rooms to paint or hang wallpaper, and I just feel like I won't be able to move around the way I need to for work if I put on weight. So... yeah, I avoid it on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be okay with gaining a little weight if it didn't affect your ability to work?\n\nPatient: I... I don't think so. Even if you could somehow guarantee it wouldn't make my job harder, the thought of gaining weight still really bothers me. I just don't want to be bigger, honestly. So, no, I don't think I would be okay with it.\n\nDoctor: What is your current weight?\n\nPatient: I'm not exactly sure what the number on the scale is right now, to be honest. I just know that my work pants hang pretty loose on me these days. People on the job sites keep making comments, saying I look too thin, but... I don't really see it that way. I just see it more like I've leaned out from staying on my feet and working hard all day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are underweight?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no. I don't really think my weight is some emergency like everyone keeps acting. To me, it just feels like they're making a big deal out of me being disciplined and staying busy with my jobs.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as others describe you, as too thin?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. When I look in the mirror, I don't see what they're seeing. I don't see someone who is too thin. I just see... me. Honestly, I still look at myself and see areas that could probably be a bit firmer, or toned up. So no, I definitely don't see this overly skinny person they keep talking about. I just see someone who works hard.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight something that you think about frequently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do think about it pretty often. It's just... if I eat something heavy, like a big sandwich or some pizza, I get this really panicky feeling. I start worrying that I'll wake up the next day bigger, or just feeling slower. I'm up and down ladders all day, painting and hauling gear, and I need to be able to move around these rooms the way I need to. So, yeah, the thought of putting on weight and not being light on my feet... it definitely scares me.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have. My work pants hang pretty loose on me now, and... well, people on the job sites keep saying I look too thin lately. But I don't really see it that way. To me, it's more like I've just leaned out from staying on my feet all day working.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the way you think it is, or is it different from how others see it, because of how you feel about your body?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how to answer that. I just think I have a different standard for myself than they do. I know my body and how I need it to perform for work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because of how you think you would look, or is it solely because of how you think it would affect your work?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, honestly. I feel like we just talked about this. It's not really about how I look to myself in the mirror. Like I said, it's more about the physical feeling and what it means for my job. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I'm here because the regular clinic doctor got on my case about my eating. I don't really think it's a huge issue, but... yeah, they wanted me to come in. \n\nMost days I just keep it simple. I usually just have coffee in the morning, maybe a few bites of something between painting jobs, and then a small dinner if I'm not too busy catching up on my class paperwork. That's pretty much it.\n\nDoctor: Do you choose to eat this way or do you often find you're just not hungry?\n\nPatient: I guess it's more of a choice. I mean, I do feel hungry sometimes, but I choose to keep it light. \n\nIf I eat something heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I get this panicky feeling. I just start worrying that I'll wake up bigger and slower. I'm up and down ladders all day, moving around rooms to paint or hang wallpaper, and I just feel like I won't be able to move around the way I need to for work if I put on weight. So... yeah, I avoid it on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be okay with gaining a little weight if it didn't affect your ability to work?\n\nPatient: I... I don't think so. Even if you could somehow guarantee it wouldn't make my job harder, the thought of gaining weight still really bothers me. I just don't want to be bigger, honestly. So, no, I don't think I would be okay with it.\n\nDoctor: What is your current weight?\n\nPatient: I'm not exactly sure what the number on the scale is right now, to be honest. I just know that my work pants hang pretty loose on me these days. People on the job sites keep making comments, saying I look too thin, but... I don't really see it that way. I just see it more like I've leaned out from staying on my feet and working hard all day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are underweight?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no. I don't really think my weight is some emergency like everyone keeps acting. To me, it just feels like they're making a big deal out of me being disciplined and staying busy with my jobs.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as others describe you, as too thin?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. When I look in the mirror, I don't see what they're seeing. I don't see someone who is too thin. I just see... me. Honestly, I still look at myself and see areas that could probably be a bit firmer, or toned up. So no, I definitely don't see this overly skinny person they keep talking about. I just see someone who works hard.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight something that you think about frequently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do think about it pretty often. It's just... if I eat something heavy, like a big sandwich or some pizza, I get this really panicky feeling. I start worrying that I'll wake up the next day bigger, or just feeling slower. I'm up and down ladders all day, painting and hauling gear, and I need to be able to move around these rooms the way I need to. So, yeah, the thought of putting on weight and not being light on my feet... it definitely scares me.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have. My work pants hang pretty loose on me now, and... well, people on the job sites keep saying I look too thin lately. But I don't really see it that way. To me, it's more like I've just leaned out from staying on my feet all day working.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the way you think it is, or is it different from how others see it, because of how you feel about your body?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how to answer that. I mean, I know what I see in the mirror, and I know what other people say. I guess there's a difference there, but I don't think it's because my mind is playing tricks on me or anything. I just think I have a different standard for myself than they do. I know my body and how I need it to perform for work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because of how you think you would look, or is it solely because of how you think it would affect your work?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, honestly. I haven't... I haven't really tried to separate the two. I talk a lot about work because that's what I do all day, but I don't know if it's solely about that. It's probably just all mixed together in my head. I just know the thought of putting on weight makes me anxious, but I couldn't tell you exactly which part of it scares me more.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about how you think you would look or how you think it would affect your overall appearance?\n\nPatient: I mean... aren't those kind of the same thing? How I look and my overall appearance? I don't really know how to separate that, to be honest. \n\nLike I was saying, I haven't really sat down and picked apart exactly why it bothers me. I just know I don't want to get bigger. It's just the whole idea of gaining weight that makes me uncomfortable, but I couldn't give you a specific reason about my appearance.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious about gaining weight if nobody else could see your body?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I've never... I've never really thought about a scenario where no one else could see me. \n\nI mean, I think I would probably still feel anxious, just because it's my own body and I'm the one who has to live in it and move around in it all day. But I really couldn't say for sure. It's just not something I've ever considered.\n\nDoctor: When you think about gaining weight, is it the idea of how your body would look to yourself that bothers you, or is it more about the functional aspects, like not being able to work as efficiently?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how to answer that either. Like I said, I haven't really broken it down like that in my head. \n\nI guess it's both? Or maybe neither. I just know that the thought of gaining weight makes me feel really anxious. I don't really sit there and think, \"Oh, I'm worried about how my stomach will look to me in the mirror,\" or \"I'm worried I won't be able to climb up the ladder at work.\" It's just a general, overwhelming feeling that getting bigger is bad and I can't let it happen. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I'm here because the regular clinic doctor got on my case about my eating. I don't really think it's a huge issue, but... yeah, they wanted me to come in. \n\nMost days I just keep it simple. I usually just have coffee in the morning, maybe a few bites of something between painting jobs, and then a small dinner if I'm not too busy catching up on my class paperwork. That's pretty much it.\n\nDoctor: Do you choose to eat this way or do you often find you're just not hungry?\n\nPatient: I guess it's more of a choice. I mean, I do feel hungry sometimes, but I choose to keep it light. \n\nIf I eat something heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I get this panicky feeling. I just start worrying that I'll wake up bigger and slower. I'm up and down ladders all day, moving around rooms to paint or hang wallpaper, and I just feel like I won't be able to move around the way I need to for work if I put on weight. So... yeah, I avoid it on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be okay with gaining a little weight if it didn't affect your ability to work?\n\nPatient: I... I don't think so. Even if you could somehow guarantee it wouldn't make my job harder, the thought of gaining weight still really bothers me. I just don't want to be bigger, honestly. So, no, I don't think I would be okay with it.\n\nDoctor: What is your current weight?\n\nPatient: I'm not exactly sure what the number on the scale is right now, to be honest. I just know that my work pants hang pretty loose on me these days. People on the job sites keep making comments, saying I look too thin, but... I don't really see it that way. I just see it more like I've leaned out from staying on my feet and working hard all day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are underweight?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no. I don't really think my weight is some emergency like everyone keeps acting. To me, it just feels like they're making a big deal out of me being disciplined and staying busy with my jobs.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as others describe you, as too thin?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. When I look in the mirror, I don't see what they're seeing. I don't see someone who is too thin. I just see... me. Honestly, I still look at myself and see areas that could probably be a bit firmer, or toned up. So no, I definitely don't see this overly skinny person they keep talking about. I just see someone who works hard.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight something that you think about frequently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do think about it pretty often. It's just... if I eat something heavy, like a big sandwich or some pizza, I get this really panicky feeling. I start worrying that I'll wake up the next day bigger, or just feeling slower. I'm up and down ladders all day, painting and hauling gear, and I need to be able to move around these rooms the way I need to. So, yeah, the thought of putting on weight and not being light on my feet... it definitely scares me.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have. My work pants hang pretty loose on me now, and... well, people on the job sites keep saying I look too thin lately. But I don't really see it that way. To me, it's more like I've just leaned out from staying on my feet all day working.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the way you think it is, or is it different from how others see it, because of how you feel about your body?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how to answer that. I mean, I know what I see in the mirror, and I know what other people say. I guess there's a difference there, but I don't think it's because my mind is playing tricks on me or anything. I just think I have a different standard for myself than they do. I know my body and how I need it to perform for work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because of how you think you would look, or is it solely because of how you think it would affect your work?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, honestly. I haven't... I haven't really tried to separate the two. I talk a lot about work because that's what I do all day, but I don't know if it's solely about that. It's probably just all mixed together in my head. I just know the thought of putting on weight makes me anxious, but I couldn't tell you exactly which part of it scares me more.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about how you think you would look or how you think it would affect your overall appearance?\n\nPatient: I mean... aren't those kind of the same thing? How I look and my overall appearance? I don't really know how to separate that, to be honest. \n\nLike I was saying, I haven't really sat down and picked apart exactly why it bothers me. I just know I don't want to get bigger. It's just the whole idea of gaining weight that makes me uncomfortable, but I couldn't give you a specific reason about my appearance.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious about gaining weight if nobody else could see your body?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I've never... I've never really thought about a scenario where no one else could see me. \n\nI mean, I think I would probably still feel anxious, just because it's my own body and I'm the one who has to live in it and move around in it all day. But I really couldn't say for sure. It's just not something I've ever considered.\n\nDoctor: When you think about gaining weight, is it the idea of how your body would look to yourself that bothers you, or is it more about the functional aspects, like not being able to work as efficiently?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how to answer that either. Like I said, I haven't really broken it down like that in my head. \n\nI guess it's both? Or maybe neither. I just know that the thought of gaining weight makes me feel really anxious. I don't really sit there and think, \"Oh, I'm worried about how my stomach will look to me in the mirror,\" or \"I'm worried I won't be able to climb up the ladder at work.\" It's just a general, overwhelming feeling that getting bigger is bad and I can't let it happen. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I'm here because the regular clinic doctor got on my case about my eating. I don't really think it's a huge issue, but... yeah, they wanted me to come in. \n\nMost days I just keep it simple. I usually just have coffee in the morning, maybe a few bites of something between painting jobs, and then a small dinner if I'm not too busy catching up on my class paperwork. That's pretty much it.\n\nDoctor: Do you choose to eat this way or do you often find you're just not hungry?\n\nPatient: I guess it's more of a choice. I mean, I do feel hungry sometimes, but I choose to keep it light. \n\nIf I eat something heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I get this panicky feeling. I just start worrying that I'll wake up bigger and slower. I'm up and down ladders all day, moving around rooms to paint or hang wallpaper, and I just feel like I won't be able to move around the way I need to for work if I put on weight. So... yeah, I avoid it on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be okay with gaining a little weight if it didn't affect your ability to work?\n\nPatient: I... I don't think so. Even if you could somehow guarantee it wouldn't make my job harder, the thought of gaining weight still really bothers me. I just don't want to be bigger, honestly. So, no, I don't think I would be okay with it.\n\nDoctor: What is your current weight?\n\nPatient: I'm not exactly sure what the number on the scale is right now, to be honest. I just know that my work pants hang pretty loose on me these days. People on the job sites keep making comments, saying I look too thin, but... I don't really see it that way. I just see it more like I've leaned out from staying on my feet and working hard all day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are underweight?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no. I don't really think my weight is some emergency like everyone keeps acting. To me, it just feels like they're making a big deal out of me being disciplined and staying busy with my jobs.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as others describe you, as too thin?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. When I look in the mirror, I don't see what they're seeing. I don't see someone who is too thin. I just see... me. Honestly, I still look at myself and see areas that could probably be a bit firmer, or toned up. So no, I definitely don't see this overly skinny person they keep talking about. I just see someone who works hard.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight something that you think about frequently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do think about it pretty often. It's just... if I eat something heavy, like a big sandwich or some pizza, I get this really panicky feeling. I start worrying that I'll wake up the next day bigger, or just feeling slower. I'm up and down ladders all day, painting and hauling gear, and I need to be able to move around these rooms the way I need to. So, yeah, the thought of putting on weight and not being light on my feet... it definitely scares me.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have. My work pants hang pretty loose on me now, and... well, people on the job sites keep saying I look too thin lately. But I don't really see it that way. To me, it's more like I've just leaned out from staying on my feet all day working.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the way you think it is, or is it different from how others see it, because of how you feel about your body?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how to answer that. I mean, I know what I see in the mirror, and I know what other people say. I guess there's a difference there, but I don't think it's because my mind is playing tricks on me or anything. I just think I have a different standard for myself than they do. I know my body and how I need it to perform for work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because of how you think you would look, or is it solely because of how you think it would affect your work?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, honestly. I haven't... I haven't really tried to separate the two. I talk a lot about work because that's what I do all day, but I don't know if it's solely about that. It's probably just all mixed together in my head. I just know the thought of putting on weight makes me anxious, but I couldn't tell you exactly which part of it scares me more.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about how you think you would look or how you think it would affect your overall appearance?\n\nPatient: I mean... aren't those kind of the same thing? How I look and my overall appearance? I don't really know how to separate that, to be honest. \n\nLike I was saying, I haven't really sat down and picked apart exactly why it bothers me. I just know I don't want to get bigger. It's just the whole idea of gaining weight that makes me uncomfortable, but I couldn't give you a specific reason about my appearance.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious about gaining weight if nobody else could see your body?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I've never... I've never really thought about a scenario where no one else could see me. \n\nI mean, I think I would probably still feel anxious, just because it's my own body and I'm the one who has to live in it and move around in it all day. But I really couldn't say for sure. It's just not something I've ever considered.\n\nDoctor: When you think about gaining weight, is it the idea of how your body would look to yourself that bothers you, or is it more about the functional aspects, like not being able to work as efficiently?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how to answer that either. Like I said, I haven't really broken it down like that in my head. \n\nI guess it's both? Or maybe neither. I just know that the thought of gaining weight makes me feel really anxious. I don't really sit there and think, \"Oh, I'm worried about how my stomach will look to me in the mirror,\" or \"I'm worried I won't be able to climb up the ladder at work.\" It's just a general, overwhelming feeling that getting bigger is bad and I can't let it happen. I don't really have a better way to explain it.\n\nDoctor: When you think about gaining weight, is the anxiety you feel more related to how you think your body would look or how it would affect your daily activities?\n\nPatient: I feel like you're asking me the same question again. Like I said, I don't really know how to separate those things out. I haven't thought about it in terms of how I look versus how it affects my day-to-day life. It's just a feeling of panic about gaining weight in general. I don't really have a specific reason I can point to.\n\nDoctor: When you imagine yourself gaining weight, what is the first thing that you worry will change about your appearance?\n\nPatient: I guess it's just my overall size.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I'm here because the regular clinic doctor got on my case about my eating. I don't really think it's a huge issue, but... yeah, they wanted me to come in. \n\nMost days I just keep it simple. I usually just have coffee in the morning, maybe a few bites of something between painting jobs, and then a small dinner if I'm not too busy catching up on my class paperwork. That's pretty much it.\n\nDoctor: Do you choose to eat this way or do you often find you're just not hungry?\n\nPatient: I guess it's more of a choice. I mean, I do feel hungry sometimes, but I choose to keep it light. \n\nIf I eat something heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I get this panicky feeling. I just start worrying that I'll wake up bigger and slower. I'm up and down ladders all day, moving around rooms to paint or hang wallpaper, and I just feel like I won't be able to move around the way I need to for work if I put on weight. So... yeah, I avoid it on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be okay with gaining a little weight if it didn't affect your ability to work?\n\nPatient: I... I don't think so. Even if you could somehow guarantee it wouldn't make my job harder, the thought of gaining weight still really bothers me. I just don't want to be bigger, honestly. So, no, I don't think I would be okay with it.\n\nDoctor: What is your current weight?\n\nPatient: I'm not exactly sure what the number on the scale is right now, to be honest. I just know that my work pants hang pretty loose on me these days. People on the job sites keep making comments, saying I look too thin, but... I don't really see it that way. I just see it more like I've leaned out from staying on my feet and working hard all day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are underweight?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no. I don't really think my weight is some emergency like everyone keeps acting. To me, it just feels like they're making a big deal out of me being disciplined and staying busy with my jobs.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as others describe you, as too thin?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. When I look in the mirror, I don't see what they're seeing. I don't see someone who is too thin. I just see... me. Honestly, I still look at myself and see areas that could probably be a bit firmer, or toned up. So no, I definitely don't see this overly skinny person they keep talking about. I just see someone who works hard.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight something that you think about frequently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do think about it pretty often. It's just... if I eat something heavy, like a big sandwich or some pizza, I get this really panicky feeling. I start worrying that I'll wake up the next day bigger, or just feeling slower. I'm up and down ladders all day, painting and hauling gear, and I need to be able to move around these rooms the way I need to. So, yeah, the thought of putting on weight and not being light on my feet... it definitely scares me.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have. My work pants hang pretty loose on me now, and... well, people on the job sites keep saying I look too thin lately. But I don't really see it that way. To me, it's more like I've just leaned out from staying on my feet all day working.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the way you think it is, or is it different from how others see it, because of how you feel about your body?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how to answer that. I mean, I know what I see in the mirror, and I know what other people say. I guess there's a difference there, but I don't think it's because my mind is playing tricks on me or anything. I just think I have a different standard for myself than they do. I know my body and how I need it to perform for work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because of how you think you would look, or is it solely because of how you think it would affect your work?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, honestly. I haven't... I haven't really tried to separate the two. I talk a lot about work because that's what I do all day, but I don't know if it's solely about that. It's probably just all mixed together in my head. I just know the thought of putting on weight makes me anxious, but I couldn't tell you exactly which part of it scares me more.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about how you think you would look or how you think it would affect your overall appearance?\n\nPatient: I mean... aren't those kind of the same thing? How I look and my overall appearance? I don't really know how to separate that, to be honest. \n\nLike I was saying, I haven't really sat down and picked apart exactly why it bothers me. I just know I don't want to get bigger. It's just the whole idea of gaining weight that makes me uncomfortable, but I couldn't give you a specific reason about my appearance.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious about gaining weight if nobody else could see your body?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I've never... I've never really thought about a scenario where no one else could see me. \n\nI mean, I think I would probably still feel anxious, just because it's my own body and I'm the one who has to live in it and move around in it all day. But I really couldn't say for sure. It's just not something I've ever considered.\n\nDoctor: When you think about gaining weight, is it the idea of how your body would look to yourself that bothers you, or is it more about the functional aspects, like not being able to work as efficiently?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how to answer that either. Like I said, I haven't really broken it down like that in my head. \n\nI guess it's both? Or maybe neither. I just know that the thought of gaining weight makes me feel really anxious. I don't really sit there and think, \"Oh, I'm worried about how my stomach will look to me in the mirror,\" or \"I'm worried I won't be able to climb up the ladder at work.\" It's just a general, overwhelming feeling that getting bigger is bad and I can't let it happen. I don't really have a better way to explain it.\n\nDoctor: When you think about gaining weight, is the anxiety you feel more related to how you think your body would look or how it would affect your daily activities?\n\nPatient: I feel like you're asking me the same question again. Like I said, I don't really know how to separate those things out. I haven't thought about it in terms of how I look versus how it affects my day-to-day life. It's just a feeling of panic about gaining weight in general. I don't really have a specific reason I can point to.\n\nDoctor: When you imagine yourself gaining weight, what is the first thing that you worry will change about your appearance?\n\nPatient: I guess it's just my overall size. Like, if I eat something heavy, like a big sandwich or pizza, I get this really panicky feeling that I'll wake up the next day and just be visibly bigger. \n\nAnd slower, too. It's not just one specific part of how I look, it's just being larger overall... and knowing that if I'm bigger, I won't be able to move around ladders and work in those rooms the way I need to. That's really where my mind goes first.\n\nDoctor: When you worry about gaining weight, is it because you're afraid of what you would look like in your own eyes?\n\nPatient: I feel like we just talked about this. It's not really about how I look to myself in the mirror. Like I said, it's more about the physical feeling and what it means for my job. If I eat something heavy, like a big sandwich or pizza, I just get this immediate, panicky feeling that I'm going to wake up the next day bigger and slower. And if I'm bigger and slower, I won't be able to climb my ladders or move around the rooms I'm painting the way I need to. That's the part that really scares me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety about gaining weight would decrease if you couldn't see your body?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I mean, even if I couldn't see myself, I'd still feel it, you know? I'd still feel heavier or slower when I'm trying to work. The panic isn't really about looking in a mirror. It's about that physical sensation of being weighed down and not being able to do my job right. So, no, I don't think hiding mirrors or not seeing my body would change that anxiety at all.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety about gaining weight decrease if you knew you would still look the same in the mirror?\n\nPatient: No, because like I said, it's not really about the mirror. Even if I looked exactly the same, I'd still have that fear of feeling different. If I eat something heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I get this panicky feeling that I'll wake up bigger and slower. And if I feel bigger and slower, I won't be able to move around ladders and rooms the way I need to for work. That's the real issue for me, not the reflection.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it because of your own perception of your body?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so. I mean, I'm not sure how other people see my weight or shape, to be honest. I haven't really noticed a difference because... well, like I was saying, I just don't focus on the visual part of it that much. It's really just about how heavy or slow my body actually feels when I'm trying to work. So, no, I don't think I see it differently than anyone else does.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Like, if I eat something heavy, like a big sandwich or pizza, I get this really panicky feeling that I'll wake up the next day and just be visibly bigger. \n\nAnd slower, too. It's not just one specific part of how I look, it's just being larger overall... and knowing that if I'm bigger, I won't be able to move around ladders and work in those rooms the way I need to. That's really where my mind goes first.\n\nDoctor: When you worry about gaining weight, is it because you're afraid of what you would look like in your own eyes?\n\nPatient: I feel like we just talked about this. It's not really about how I look to myself in the mirror. Like I said, it's more about the physical feeling and what it means for my job. If I eat something heavy, like a big sandwich or pizza, I just get this immediate, panicky feeling that I'm going to wake up the next day bigger and slower. And if I'm bigger and slower, I won't be able to climb my ladders or move around the rooms I'm painting the way I need to. That's the part that really scares me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety about gaining weight would decrease if you couldn't see your body?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I mean, even if I couldn't see myself, I'd still feel it, you know? I'd still feel heavier or slower when I'm trying to work. The panic isn't really about looking in a mirror. It's about that physical sensation of being weighed down and not being able to do my job right. So, no, I don't think hiding mirrors or not seeing my body would change that anxiety at all.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety about gaining weight decrease if you knew you would still look the same in the mirror?\n\nPatient: No, because like I said, it's not really about the mirror. Even if I looked exactly the same, I'd still have that fear of feeling different. If I eat something heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I get this panicky feeling that I'll wake up bigger and slower. And if I feel bigger and slower, I won't be able to move around ladders and rooms the way I need to for work. That's the real issue for me, not the reflection.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it because of your own perception of your body?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so. I mean, I'm not sure how other people see my weight or shape, to be honest. I haven't really noticed a difference because... well, like I was saying, I just don't focus on the visual part of it that much. It's really just about how heavy or slow my body actually feels when I'm trying to work. So, no, I don't think I see it differently than anyone else does.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. My work pants hang pretty loose on me now, and... well, people on the job sites keep saying I look too thin lately. But I don't really see it that way. To me, it's more like I've just leaned out from staying on my feet all day working.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the way you think it is, or is it different from how others see it, because of how you feel about your body?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how to answer that. I mean, I know what I see in the mirror, and I know what other people say. I guess there's a difference there, but I don't think it's because my mind is playing tricks on me or anything. I just think I have a different standard for myself than they do. I know my body and how I need it to perform for work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because of how you think you would look, or is it solely because of how you think it would affect your work?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, honestly. I haven't... I haven't really tried to separate the two. I talk a lot about work because that's what I do all day, but I don't know if it's solely about that. It's probably just all mixed together in my head. I just know the thought of putting on weight makes me anxious, but I couldn't tell you exactly which part of it scares me more.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about how you think you would look or how you think it would affect your overall appearance?\n\nPatient: I mean... aren't those kind of the same thing? How I look and my overall appearance? I don't really know how to separate that, to be honest. \n\nLike I was saying, I haven't really sat down and picked apart exactly why it bothers me. I just know I don't want to get bigger. It's just the whole idea of gaining weight that makes me uncomfortable, but I couldn't give you a specific reason about my appearance.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious about gaining weight if nobody else could see your body?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I've never... I've never really thought about a scenario where no one else could see me. \n\nI mean, I think I would probably still feel anxious, just because it's my own body and I'm the one who has to live in it and move around in it all day. But I really couldn't say for sure. It's just not something I've ever considered.\n\nDoctor: When you think about gaining weight, is it the idea of how your body would look to yourself that bothers you, or is it more about the functional aspects, like not being able to work as efficiently?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how to answer that either. Like I said, I haven't really broken it down like that in my head. \n\nI guess it's both? Or maybe neither. I just know that the thought of gaining weight makes me feel really anxious. I don't really sit there and think, \"Oh, I'm worried about how my stomach will look to me in the mirror,\" or \"I'm worried I won't be able to climb up the ladder at work.\" It's just a general, overwhelming feeling that getting bigger is bad and I can't let it happen. I don't really have a better way to explain it.\n\nDoctor: When you think about gaining weight, is the anxiety you feel more related to how you think your body would look or how it would affect your daily activities?\n\nPatient: I feel like you're asking me the same question again. Like I said, I don't really know how to separate those things out. I haven't thought about it in terms of how I look versus how it affects my day-to-day life. It's just a feeling of panic about gaining weight in general. I don't really have a specific reason I can point to.\n\nDoctor: When you imagine yourself gaining weight, what is the first thing that you worry will change about your appearance?\n\nPatient: I guess it's just my overall size. Like, if I eat something heavy, like a big sandwich or pizza, I get this really panicky feeling that I'll wake up the next day and just be visibly bigger. \n\nAnd slower, too. It's not just one specific part of how I look, it's just being larger overall... and knowing that if I'm bigger, I won't be able to move around ladders and work in those rooms the way I need to. That's really where my mind goes first.\n\nDoctor: When you worry about gaining weight, is it because you're afraid of what you would look like in your own eyes?\n\nPatient: I feel like we just talked about this. It's not really about how I look to myself in the mirror. Like I said, it's more about the physical feeling and what it means for my job. If I eat something heavy, like a big sandwich or pizza, I just get this immediate, panicky feeling that I'm going to wake up the next day bigger and slower. And if I'm bigger and slower, I won't be able to climb my ladders or move around the rooms I'm painting the way I need to. That's the part that really scares me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety about gaining weight would decrease if you couldn't see your body?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I mean, even if I couldn't see myself, I'd still feel it, you know? I'd still feel heavier or slower when I'm trying to work. The panic isn't really about looking in a mirror. It's about that physical sensation of being weighed down and not being able to do my job right. So, no, I don't think hiding mirrors or not seeing my body would change that anxiety at all.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety about gaining weight decrease if you knew you would still look the same in the mirror?\n\nPatient: No, because like I said, it's not really about the mirror. Even if I looked exactly the same, I'd still have that fear of feeling different. If I eat something heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I get this panicky feeling that I'll wake up bigger and slower. And if I feel bigger and slower, I won't be able to move around ladders and rooms the way I need to for work. That's the real issue for me, not the reflection.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it because of your own perception of your body?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so. I mean, I'm not sure how other people see my weight or shape, to be honest. I haven't really noticed a difference because... well, like I was saying, I just don't focus on the visual part of it that much. It's really just about how heavy or slow my body actually feels when I'm trying to work. So, no, I don't think I see it differently than anyone else does.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your anxiety about gaining weight is more about how your body would feel or how it would look to you?\n\nPatient: Definitely how it feels. Um, like I've been saying, the visual part just isn't what bothers me. It's entirely about that physical sensation of being heavy or slow. If I feel weighed down, it messes with my work, so... yeah, it's strictly about how my body feels to me, not how it looks."
